\setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_\gamma $$\end{document}Jγ in B, if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {J}_{\gamma }(u,B)\le \mathcal {J}_{\gamma }(v, B),\qquad \text {for all } v\in H^1(B) \text { such that } v-u \in H^1_0(B). $$\end{document}Jγ(u,B)≤Jγ(v,B),for allv∈H1(B)such thatv-u∈H01(B).Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u: \mathbb {R}^d\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}u:Rd→R is said to be a global minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_\gamma $$\end{document}Jγ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd (resp. global stable solution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd) if u is a local minimizer (resp. stable solution) in every ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\subset \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}B⊂Rd. The previous definitions can be naturally extended to the function w.

Second inner variation

The following result, is our first characterization of the stability condition, which ultimately implies Theorem 1.3.

Proposition 3.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0), and B be a ball in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in C^\infty _c(B)$$\end{document}f∈Cc∞(B) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u:B\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}u:B→R be a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_\gamma $$\end{document}Jγ in B; or a stable solution in B, in the sense of Definition 3.1. Assume that u is regular in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), in the sense of Definition 2.1. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{ \Omega _u} |\nabla u|^2 \left( |\nabla f|^2 - \mathcal {A}^2_u\, f^2\right) \,dx\ge 0,\quad \text {where }\mathcal {A}^2_u{:}{=} \frac{|\nabla ^2 u|^2}{|\nabla u|^2} - \frac{|\nabla ^2 u\nabla u|^2}{|\nabla u|^4}. $$\end{document}∫Ωu|∇u|2|∇f|2-Au2f2dx≥0,whereAu2:=|∇2u|2|∇u|2-|∇2u∇u|2|∇u|4.Equivalently, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w:B\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}w:B→R be a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es in B; or a stable solution in B in the sense of Definition 3.1. Assume that u is regular in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), in the sense of Definition 2.1. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{ \Omega _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2 \left( |\nabla f|^2 - \mathcal {A}^2_w\, f^2\right) \,dx\ge 0,\quad \text {where }\mathcal {A}^2_w{:}{=} \frac{|\nabla ^2 w|^2}{|\nabla w|^2} - \frac{|\nabla ^2 w\nabla w|^2}{|\nabla w|^4}. $$\end{document}∫Ωwws|∇w|2|∇f|2-Aw2f2dx≥0,whereAw2:=|∇2w|2|∇w|2-|∇2w∇w|2|∇w|4.

In order to compute a second order expansion close to regular free boundaries, we work directly with local minimizers w of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es (resp. stable solutions) being smooth close to regular points. Throughout the section, given a vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi :\mathbb {R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}ξ:Rd→Rd we denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\xi \in \mathbb {R}^{d\times d}$$\end{document}Dξ∈Rd×d the matrix with entries \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(D\xi )_{ij}{:}{=}\partial _i \xi _j$$\end{document}(Dξ)ij:=∂iξj, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le d$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤d.

In the following result we compute the first variation of the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es along inner variations. Ultimately, we observe that regular solutions are indeed stationary along non-tangential variations. We stress that such computation is not necessary for the proof of Proposition 3.3.

Lemma 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0), and B be a ball in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd. \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es at w with respect to ξ ξ the quantity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \delta \mathcal {E}_s(w,B)[\xi ]{:}{=}\frac{d}{d t}\bigg |_{t=0}\mathcal {E}_s(w \circ \Phi _t^{-1},B),\quad \text{ where } \Phi _t(x){:}{=}x+t\xi (x). $$\end{document}δEs(w,B)[ξ]:=ddt|t=0Es(w∘Φt-1,B),whereΦt(x):=x+tξ(x).Then3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta \mathcal {E}_s(w,B)[\xi ]=\int _{\Omega _w}w^s\Big ((|\nabla \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^\infty _c(B)$$\end{document}f∈Cc∞(B), if w is a regular solution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), in the sense of Definition 2.1, we have3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta \mathcal {E}_s(w,B)\left[ \frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|} f\right] = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δEs(w,B)∇w|∇w|f=0.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in C^\infty _c(B;\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}ξ∈Cc∞(B;Rd) be a smooth vector field with compact support in B and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t{:}{=}\textrm{Id}+t\xi $$\end{document}Φt:=Id+tξ. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_t:B\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}wt:B→R be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_t(x){:}{=}w(\Phi ^{-1}_t(x))$$\end{document}wt(x):=w(Φt-1(x)). Then, by the change of variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=\Phi _t(x)$$\end{document}y=Φt(x), we get3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathcal {E}_s(w \circ \Phi _t^{-1},B) =\int _{\Omega _{w_t}}w_t^s(|\nabla w_t|^2+1)\,dx\\&\quad =\int _{\Omega _w} w^s(\Phi _t^{-1}(x))\Big (\big |D\Phi _t^{-1}(x)\nabla w_t(\Phi _t^{-1}(x))\big |^2+1\Big )\,dx\\&\quad =\int _{\Omega _w}w^s(y)\Big (D\Phi _t^{-1}(\Phi _t(y))^T D\Phi _t^{-1}(\Phi _t(y))\nabla w(y)\cdot \nabla w(y)+1\Big )|\text {det}D\Phi _t(y)|\,dy\\&\quad =\int _{\Omega _w}w^s(y)\Big ([D\Phi _t(y)]^{-T}[D\Phi _t(y)]^{-1}\nabla w(y)\cdot \nabla w(y)+1\Big )|\text {det}D\Phi _t(y)|\,dy. \end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Es(w∘Φt-1,B)=∫Ωwtwts(|∇wt|2+1)dx=∫Ωwws(Φt-1(x))(|DΦt-1(x)∇wt(Φt-1(x))|2+1)dx=∫Ωwws(y)(DΦt-1(Φt(y))TDΦt-1(Φt(y))∇w(y)·∇w(y)+1)|detDΦt(y)|dy=∫Ωwws(y)([DΦt(y)]-T[DΦt(y)]-1∇w(y)·∇w(y)+1)|detDΦt(y)|dy.Using the facts that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D\Phi _t^{-1}=\textrm{Id}-tD\xi + o(t)\qquad \text {and}\qquad |\text {det}D\Phi _t|=1+t{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\xi + o(t), $$\end{document}DΦt-1=Id-tDξ+o(t)and|detDΦt|=1+tdivξ+o(t),we can expand the previous identity to first order in t, obtaining (3.1), as we claimed.

Now, suppose that w is a regular solution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), in the sense of Definition 2.1. By exploiting the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,\alpha }$$\end{document}C1,α-regularity of both w and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw (for some α >-sα>-s) we can rewrite (3.1) by integrating by parts. By the Rellich-type identity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\Big (|\nabla w|^2\xi -2(\xi \cdot \nabla w)\nabla w\Big )=|\nabla w|^2{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\xi -2D\xi \nabla w\cdot \nabla w-2\Delta w(\xi \cdot \nabla w), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}div(|∇w|2ξ-2(ξ·∇w)∇w)=|∇w|2divξ-2Dξ∇w·∇w-2Δw(ξ·∇w),we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}&\delta \mathcal {E}_s(w,B)[\xi ]= \int _{\Omega _w}\left[ w^s{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\Big ((|\nabla w|^2+1)\xi -2(\xi \cdot \nabla w)\nabla w\Big )+2w^s\Delta w(\xi \cdot \nabla w)\right] \,dx \\&\quad = \int _{\Omega _w} \textrm{div}\Big ( w^s\,(|\nabla w|^2+1)\xi -2w^s(\xi \cdot \nabla w)\nabla w\Big )\, dx \\&\quad \quad +2\int _{\Omega _w} w^s\Delta w(\xi \cdot \nabla w) - \frac{s}{2}w^{s-1}\nabla w\cdot \Big ((|\nabla w|^2+1)\xi -2(\xi \cdot \nabla w)\nabla w\Big )\,dx \\&\quad = \int _{\Omega _w} \textrm{div}\Big ( w^s\,(1-|\nabla w|^2)\xi +2w^s|\nabla w|^2\xi -2w^s(\xi \cdot \nabla w)\nabla w\Big )\, dx \\&\quad \quad +2\int _{\Omega _w} w^s\left( \Delta w - \frac{s}{2}\frac{1-|\nabla w|^2}{w} \right) (\xi \cdot \nabla w) \,dx. \end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}δEs(w,B)[ξ]=∫Ωwwsdiv((|∇w|2+1)ξ-2(ξ·∇w)∇w)+2wsΔw(ξ·∇w)dx=∫Ωwdiv(ws(|∇w|2+1)ξ-2ws(ξ·∇w)∇w)dx+2∫ΩwwsΔw(ξ·∇w)-s2ws-1∇w·((|∇w|2+1)ξ-2(ξ·∇w)∇w)dx=∫Ωwdiv(ws(1-|∇w|2)ξ+2ws|∇w|2ξ-2ws(ξ·∇w)∇w)dx+2∫ΩwwsΔw-s21-|∇w|2w(ξ·∇w)dx.By substituting (2.1), we already know that the last integral is null for every smooth vector field with compact support. On the other hand, for non-tangential variations, the previous computation can be further improved. Thus, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^\infty _c(B)$$\end{document}f∈Cc∞(B), we consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \xi := \frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|} f. $$\end{document}ξ:=∇w|∇w|f.Indeed, since in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w \cap B$$\end{document}∂Ωw∩B the interior normal coincides with the gradient of w, the previous variation is perpendicular to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw as well. Then, by direct substitution, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\delta \mathcal {E}_s(w,B)\left[ \frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}f\right] = \int _{\Omega _w} \textrm{div}\bigg ( w^s\,(1-|\nabla w|^2)\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}f\bigg )\, dx \\&\quad =\int _{\partial \Omega _w} w^s\,(|\nabla w|^2-1)f \, d\mathcal {H}^{d-1} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δEs(w,B)∇w|∇w|f=∫Ωwdiv(ws(1-|∇w|2)∇w|∇w|f)dx=∫∂Ωwws(|∇w|2-1)fdHd-1where in the last equality we exploit the Gauss–Green formula as in [30, Proposition 2.7]. Precisely, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w \in C^{1,\alpha }(\overline{\Omega }_w\cap \text {supp}(f))$$\end{document}w∈C1,α(Ω¯w∩supp(f)) for some α >-sα>-s, it implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^s(|\nabla w|^2-1)$$\end{document}ws(|∇w|2-1) extends continuously to zero on the free boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw. Finally, (3.2) follows by the free boundary condition in (2.1). □ □

We proceed now to the study of the second variation.

Lemma 3.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0), and B be a ball in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^\infty _c(B)$$\end{document}f∈Cc∞(B) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w:B\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}w:B→R be a regular solution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), in the sense of Definition 2.1. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta ^2\mathcal {E}_s(w,B)\left[ \frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}f\right] =\int _{\Omega _w}(f_1+f_2+f_3)\,dx, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δ2Es(w,B)∇w|∇w|f=∫Ωw(f1+f2+f3)dx,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1:=\frac{1}{2}w^s\Big (|\nabla w|^2+1\Big ){{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\left[ \left( \frac{\Delta w\nabla w}{|\nabla w|^2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nabla (|\nabla w|^2)}{|\nabla w|^2}\right) f^2 \right] , $$\end{document}f1:=12ws(|∇w|2+1)divΔw∇w|∇w|2-12∇(|∇w|2)|∇w|2f2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned} f_2&:=w^s|\nabla f|^2|\nabla w|^2, \end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}f2:=ws|∇f|2|∇w|2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_3:=w^s\Big ((\Delta w)^2-|\nabla ^2w|^2\Big )f^2+w^s{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\left[ \left( \frac{1}{2}\nabla (|\nabla w|^2)-\Delta w\nabla w\right) f^2\right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f3:=ws((Δw)2-|∇2w|2)f2+wsdiv12∇(|∇w|2)-Δw∇wf2.

Proof

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in C^\infty _c(B;\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}ξ∈Cc∞(B;Rd) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t:=\textrm{Id} + t \xi $$\end{document}Φt:=Id+tξ, we consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_t:B\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}wt:B→R be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_t(x):=w(\Phi ^{-1}_t(x))$$\end{document}wt(x):=w(Φt-1(x)). By (3.3) and the following second order expansions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D\Phi _t^{-1}&=\textrm{Id}-tD\xi +t^2[D\xi ]^2+ o(t^2),\\|\text {det}D\Phi _t|&=1+t{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\xi + \frac{t^2}{2}\Big (({{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\xi )^2-\text {Tr}(D\xi )^2\Big )+o(t^2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DΦt-1=Id-tDξ+t2[Dξ]2+o(t2),|detDΦt|=1+tdivξ+t22((divξ)2-Tr(Dξ)2)+o(t2),we have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned} \delta ^2 \mathcal {E}_s(w)[\xi ]&=\int _{\Omega _w} w^s\bigg (\Big (|\nabla w|^2+1\Big )\frac{({{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\xi )^2-\text {Tr}(D\xi )^2}{2}+|D\xi \nabla w|^2\\ &\quad +2\Big ([D\xi ]^2\nabla w\cdot \nabla w-D\xi \nabla w\cdot \nabla w {{\,\textrm{div}\,}}\xi \Big )\bigg )\,dx.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}δ2Es(w)[ξ]=∫Ωwws((|∇w|2+1)(divξ)2-Tr(Dξ)22+|Dξ∇w|2+2([Dξ]2∇w·∇w-Dξ∇w·∇wdivξ))dx.Now, set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f3:=2ws([Dξ]2∇w·∇w-Dξ∇w·∇wdivξ).Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} For what it concerns \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1$$\end{document}f1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_3$$\end{document}f3, we proceed by using some general differential identities, already exploited in [33, Subsection 4.2] for the study of the Alt–Phillips functional for positive exponents. Lemma 3.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0), and B be a ball in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^\infty _c(B)$$\end{document}f∈Cc∞(B) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w:B\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}w:B→R be a local minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es in B; or a stable solution in B, in the sense of Definition 3.1. Assume that w is regular in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), in the sense of Definition 2.1. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1$$\end{document}f1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_3$$\end{document}f3 are as in Lemma 3.5, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{\Omega _w}(f_1+f_3)\,dx=-\int _{\Omega _w}w^s|\nabla w|^2\mathcal {A}_w^2f^2\,dx,\quad \text{ where } \mathcal {A}_w^2=\frac{|\nabla ^2w|^2}{|\nabla w|^2}-\frac{|\nabla ^2w\nabla w|^2}{|\nabla w|^4}. $$\end{document}∫Ωw(f1+f3)dx=-∫Ωwws|∇w|2Aw2f2dx,whereAw2=|∇2w|2|∇w|2-|∇2w∇w|2|∇w|4.

Proof

Since w is a regular solution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,\alpha }$$\end{document}C1,α-regular free boundary in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f), then, by Proposition 2.2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ w\in C^{\infty }(\overline{\Omega }_w\cap \text {supp}(f)) \text { and the free boundary } \partial \Omega _w \text { is smooth in } \text {supp}(f). $$\end{document}w∈C∞(Ω¯w∩supp(f))and the free boundary∂Ωwis smooth in supp(f).Expanding the divergence of the second term in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Hence, as in the proof of Lemma 3.4 we can apply the Gauss–Green formula as in [30, Proposition 2.7]. In fact, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in C^{\infty }(\overline{\Omega }_w\cap \text {supp}(f))$$\end{document}w∈C∞(Ω¯w∩supp(f)), the vector field F can be extended continuously to zero on the free boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}supp(f) and so F is divergence free in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _w\cap \text {supp}(f)$$\end{document}Ωw∩supp(f). Then the result follows integrating the last identity and using the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_w^2$$\end{document}Aw2. □ □

We conclude with the proof of the stability condition in Theorem 1.3 and Proposition 3.3.

Proof of Proposition 3.3

It follows immediately by combining Lemma 3.5 and Lemma 3.6. □ □

Proof of Theorem 1.3

Since the free boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw is a smooth cone outside the origin, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d\setminus \{0\})$$\end{document}f∈Cc∞(Rd\{0}) we can apply Proposition 3.3 to conclude. □ □

Remark 3.7

(Stability condition for stable solutions) Using Proposition 3.3, we observe that Theorem 1.3 holds even for global stable solutions u of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_\gamma $$\end{document}Jγ, regular outside the origin, in the sense of Definition 3.2 and Definition 2.1. Similarly, Theorem 1.3 holds for w under the corresponding assumptions.

Remark 3.8

(Stability conditions for general exponents) Recently in [33], the authors obtained a stability condition for the Alt–Phillips problem with positive exponents, by computing the second variations of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ t \mapsto \mathcal {E}_s\left( w\circ (\textrm{Id}+t \xi )^{-1}\right) ,\quad \text{ where } \xi :=\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|^2}\varphi , $$\end{document}t↦Esw∘(Id+tξ)-1,whereξ:=∇w|∇w|2φ,and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d \setminus \{0\})$$\end{document}φ∈Cc∞(Rd\{0}). Their condition has a different form from our (1.5) and is given by3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega _w}w^s|\nabla \varphi |^2\,dx\ge \int _{\Omega _w}w^s\frac{\Delta w}{w}\varphi ^2\,dx,\quad \text{ for } \text{ every } \varphi \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d {\setminus } \{0\}).\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωwws|∇φ|2dx≥∫ΩwwsΔwwφ2dx,foreveryφ∈Cc∞(Rd\{0}).The choice of such variations is natural and it coincides with the one considered by Caffarelli, Jerison and Kenig in [7], for the Alt–Caffarelli problem (γ =0γ=0), where they show that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{\Omega _w}|\nabla \varphi |^2\,dx\ge \int _{\partial \Omega _w}H\varphi ^2\,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1},\quad \text{ for } \text{ every } \varphi \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d {\setminus } \{0\}). $$\end{document}∫Ωw|∇φ|2dx≥∫∂ΩwHφ2dHd-1,foreveryφ∈Cc∞(Rd\{0}).Nevertheless, it is well-known that this latter inequality, can be also written in a Sternberg–Zumbrun form [46]. Indeed, by choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi :=|\nabla w|f$$\end{document}φ:=|∇w|f and integrating by parts, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{ \Omega _w} |\nabla w|^2\left( |\nabla f|^2 - \mathcal {A}^2_w\, f^2\right) \,dx \ge 0,\quad \text{ where } \mathcal {A}^2_w:= \frac{|\nabla ^2 w|^2}{|\nabla w|^2} - \frac{|\nabla ^2 w\nabla w|^2}{|\nabla w|^4}, $$\end{document}∫Ωw|∇w|2|∇f|2-Aw2f2dx≥0,whereAw2:=|∇2w|2|∇w|2-|∇2w∇w|2|∇w|4,which is exactly Theorem 1.3 in the case s=0s=0 (i.e., γ =0γ=0). Naturally, a similar formulation holds for the case of positive exponents.

Surprisingly, in the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0), the presence of the singular weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^s$$\end{document}ws prevents to write both the conditions. Heuristically, the stability condition can be computed by considering only variations of the form (1.5), which are more close to the one exploited in the study of stable minimal surfaces. Indeed, by comparing the stability condition (4.3) below with the one for positive exponents (3.4), we observe that in the negative regime, it is not possible to split the integrals and integrate by parts the divergence term in (4.3), since the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^s$$\end{document}ws is singular on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw. Moreover, for negative exponents, such integration can be computed if and only if the mean curvature of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega _w$$\end{document}∂Ωw is identically zero.

Axially symmetric cones

In this section, we consider the specific case of minimizing axially symmetric cones, namely homogeneous minimizers u that are invariant under rotations around a fixed axis. Precisely, we say that u is axially symmetric if, up to a rotation, we have that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x =(x',x_d)\in \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}x=(x′,xd)∈Rd\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(x',x_d)=u(\tau ,x_d),\quad \text {where } \tau :=|x'|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(x′,xd)=u(τ,xd),whereτ:=|x′|.The main result of the section is Theorem 1.4, namely that minimizing axially symmetric cones are one-dimensional in low dimensions.

Before presenting the proof of the main result, we begin by rephrasing the stability condition under axial symmetry as an Hardy-type inequality. For simplicity of notation, we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\tau :=\nabla u\cdot \frac{(x',0)}{\tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uτ:=∇u·(x′,0)τ.The same notations apply to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w:=\beta u^{1/\beta }$$\end{document}w:=βu1/β. The following is the main result towards the proof of Theorem 1.4.

Proposition 4.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0), d≥ 3d≥3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^{0,\beta }(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}u∈C0,β(Rd) be a β β-homogeneous global minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}_{\gamma }$$\end{document}Jγ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd; or a global stable solution in the sense of Definition 3.2. Assume that u is axially symmetric and that is regular outside the origin, in the sense of Definition 2.1. Then4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega _u} u_\tau ^2 \left( |\nabla \eta |^2-(d-2)\frac{\eta ^2}{\tau ^2}\right) \,dx\ge 0,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωuuτ2|∇η|2-(d-2)η2τ2dx≥0,for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}η∈Cc∞(Rd).

Equivalently, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}w∈C0,1(Rd) be a 1-homogeneous global minimizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_{s}$$\end{document}Es in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd; or a global stable solution, in the sense of Definition 3.2. Assume that w is axially symmetric and that is regular outside the origin, in the sense of Definition 2.1. Then4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega _w}w^s w_\tau ^2 \left( |\nabla \eta |^2-(d-2)\frac{\eta ^2}{\tau ^2}\right) \,dx\ge 0,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωwwswτ2|∇η|2-(d-2)η2τ2dx≥0,for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}η∈Cc∞(Rd).

The proof of Proposition 4.1 is carried out by working directly with w and proving (4.2), from which (4.1) immediately follows. The key point is to use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:=\frac{1}{|\nabla w|}w_\tau \eta $$\end{document}f:=1|∇w|wτη as a test function in the stability condition of Theorem 1.3, where η η is a cut-off function with compact support in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd. We stress, in relation to (1.5), that such a test function corresponds to testing the second variation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es with the vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi =\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|^2}w_\tau \eta . $$\end{document}ξ=∇w|∇w|2wτη.

We proceed directly with the proof of Proposition 4.1 (see also [33] for a similar result in the context of positive exponents).

Proof of Proposition 4.1

Step 3. This extension follows by standard arguments (see e.g., [24, 33]). Precisely, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}η∈Cc∞(Rd) we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _\varepsilon :=\eta \zeta _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ηε:=ηζε, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ζε is first chosen as an axially symmetric cut-off function, and subsequently replaced by a radial one. Precisely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \in C^\infty _c(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}ζ∈Cc∞(R) is the one dimensional cut-off function such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \equiv 0$$\end{document}ζ≡0 in (-∞ ,1/2)(-∞,1/2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \equiv 1$$\end{document}ζ≡1 in (1,+∞ )(1,+∞), then we set first \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _\varepsilon =\zeta (\tau /\varepsilon )$$\end{document}ζε=ζ(τ/ε) and subsequently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _\varepsilon =\zeta (|x|/\varepsilon )$$\end{document}ζε=ζ(|x|/ε). Since by regularity of w and the axially symmetric property, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^sw_\tau ^2\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}wswτ2→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}τ→0+, then sending \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}ε→0+ (here we are using the assumption that d≥ 3d≥3), we conclude the stability inequality (4.2) for η η. □ □

Now we are ready to prove Theorem 1.4.

Then, using (4.4) and (4.5), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int _{\left( B_{2R}{\setminus } B_R\right) \cap \{\tau>\varepsilon \}}w^sw_\tau ^2\tau ^{-2\theta }|\nabla \zeta _R|^2\,dx&\le CR^{s-2}\int _{\left( B_{2R}{\setminus } B_R\right) \cap \{\tau >\varepsilon \}}\tau ^{-2\theta }\,dx\\ &\le CR^{s-1}\int _{\varepsilon }^{2R}\tau ^{d-2-2\theta }\,d\tau \\ &=CR^{s-1}\left( R^{d-1-2\theta }-\varepsilon ^{d-1-2\theta }\right) . \end{aligned}\end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫B2R\BR∩{τ>ε}wswτ2τ-2θ|∇ζR|2dx≤CRs-2∫B2R\BR∩{τ>ε}τ-2θdx≤CRs-1∫ε2Rτd-2-2θdτ=CRs-1Rd-1-2θ-εd-1-2θ.

(ii)For the second integral, by (4.4) and (4.5), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int _{B_{2R}\cap \{\tau \le \varepsilon \}}w^sw_\tau ^2\varepsilon ^{-2\theta }|\nabla \zeta _R|^2\,dx\le CR^{s-2}\varepsilon ^{-2\theta }\int _{B_{2R}\cap \{\tau \le \varepsilon \}}\,dx= CR^{s-1}\varepsilon ^{d-1-2\theta }.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫B2R∩{τ≤ε}wswτ2ε-2θ|∇ζR|2dx≤CRs-2ε-2θ∫B2R∩{τ≤ε}dx=CRs-1εd-1-2θ.

For the second integral, by (4.4) and (4.5), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int _{B_{2R}\cap \{\tau \le \varepsilon \}}w^sw_\tau ^2\varepsilon ^{-2\theta }|\nabla \zeta _R|^2\,dx\le CR^{s-2}\varepsilon ^{-2\theta }\int _{B_{2R}\cap \{\tau \le \varepsilon \}}\,dx= CR^{s-1}\varepsilon ^{d-1-2\theta }.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫B2R∩{τ≤ε}wswτ2ε-2θ|∇ζR|2dx≤CRs-2ε-2θ∫B2R∩{τ≤ε}dx=CRs-1εd-1-2θ.

Remark 4.2

(On the homogeneity assumption) In the proof of Theorem 1.4, the homogeneity assumption is in fact unnecessary. It was only used to derive the estimate (4.5). However, this bound actually follows from the regularity of the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^{0,\beta }(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}u∈C0,β(Rd), together with the identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\tau ^2=\beta ^{-s}w^sw_\tau ^2$$\end{document}uτ2=β-swswτ2.

Asymptotic of stable cones as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow -2$$\end{document}γ→-2

In this section, we begin by providing a variational characterization of the stability condition for homogeneous global solutions, formulated as an eigenvalue problem for a weighted Laplace-Beltrami operator. Ultimately, we provide some insight into the limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow -2$$\end{document}γ→-2, by showing the the convergence of this stability criterion to that associated with stable minimal cones.

A stability criterion for cones

In the context of homogeneous solutions with isolated singularities, a stability condition can be reformulated as a lower bound for an eigenvalue problem associated with a Laplace-Beltrami operator on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1. As already observed in [31] for γ = 0γ=0 (i.e., the Alt–Caffarelli problem), and in [6, 44] for the limiting case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow -2$$\end{document}γ→-2 (i.e., the minimal surfaces), this criterion serves as a key tool in ruling out the presence of singularities for homogeneous free boundaries (see also [39] for the analogue in the capillary context).

The following result is the analogue of these criteria for the Alt–Phillips problem, where the eigenvalue problem involves a degenerate Laplace-Beltrami operator on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1. For clarity, it is stated in terms of the auxiliary function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w:= \beta u^{1/\beta }$$\end{document}w:=βu1/β, although it can be equivalently formulated in terms of u. In the following, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _S$$\end{document}∇S and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textrm{div}_S$$\end{document}divS respectively the tangential gradient and the tangential divergence on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1.

Proposition 5.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,0)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w \in C^{0,1}(\mathbb {R}^d)$$\end{document}w∈C0,1(Rd) be a 1-homogeneous global regular solution outside the origin, in the sense of Definition 2.1. Given the regular section \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _w:=\Omega _w \cap \mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Σw:=Ωw∩Sd-1, consider the degenerate eigenvalue problem on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-15.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _s(\Sigma _w):= \min _{\begin{array}{c} \varphi \in C^\infty (\mathbb {S}^{d-1})\\ \varphi \not \equiv 0 \end{array}}\frac{\displaystyle \int _{\Sigma _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2\big (|\nabla _S \varphi |^2 - \mathcal {A}_w^2 \varphi ^2\big )\,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1}}{\displaystyle \int _{\Sigma _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2 \varphi ^2 \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1}}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λs(Σw):=minφ∈C∞(Sd-1)φ≢0∫Σwws|∇w|2(|∇Sφ|2-Aw2φ2)dHd-1∫Σwws|∇w|2φ2dHd-1,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^2_w$$\end{document}Aw2 as in (1.4). Then, u is stable in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd if and only if5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _s(\Sigma _w)\ge -\left( \frac{d+s-2}{2}\right) ^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λs(Σw)≥-d+s-222

Proof

Throughout the proof, we often identity a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}x∈Rd with its spherical coordinates (r,θ )(r,θ), with r>0r>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}θ∈Sd-1. Notice also that, since w is a 1-homogeneous function, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^s$$\end{document}ws is s-homogeneous, |∇ w||∇w| is 0-homogeneous and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_w^2$$\end{document}Aw2 is (-2)(-2)-homogeneous.

Thus, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(r,\theta ):=g(r)\varphi (\theta )$$\end{document}f(r,θ):=g(r)φ(θ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C^\infty (\mathbb {S}^{d-1})$$\end{document}φ∈C∞(Sd-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in C^\infty _c((0,+\infty ))$$\end{document}g∈Cc∞((0,+∞)). Using the coarea formula and the homogeneity of w, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega _w} w^s |\nabla w|^2 \left( |\nabla f|^2 -\mathcal {A}^2_w f^2\right) \,dx&=\int _0^{\infty } r^{s+d-3}g^2(r)\,dr \int _{\Sigma _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2\\&\quad \left( |\nabla _S \varphi |^2 - \mathcal {A}_w^2\varphi ^2\right) \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1}\\&\quad + \int _0^{\infty } r^{s+d-1}(g'(r))^2\,dr \int _{\Sigma _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2 \varphi ^2 \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωwws|∇w|2|∇f|2-Aw2f2dx=∫0∞rs+d-3g2(r)dr∫Σwws|∇w|2|∇Sφ|2-Aw2φ2dHd-1+∫0∞rs+d-1(g′(r))2dr∫Σwws|∇w|2φ2dHd-1.On the other hand, since the Hardy’s inequality implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \inf \left\{ \frac{\int _0^\infty r^{s+d-1}(g'(r))^2\,dr}{\int _0^\infty r^{s+d-3}g(r)^2\,dr} : g \in C^\infty _c((0,+\infty ))\right\} = \left( \frac{d+s-2}{2}\right) ^2, $$\end{document}inf∫0∞rs+d-1(g′(r))2dr∫0∞rs+d-3g(r)2dr:g∈Cc∞((0,+∞))=d+s-222,we deduce that, for 1-homogeneous solutions, the stability condition (1.4) holds if and only if5.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Sigma _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2\left( |\nabla _S \varphi |^2 - \mathcal {A}_w^2\varphi ^2\right) \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1} + \left( \frac{d+s-2}{2}\right) ^2 \int _{\Sigma _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2\varphi ^2 \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-1}\ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Σwws|∇w|2|∇Sφ|2-Aw2φ2dHd-1+d+s-222∫Σwws|∇w|2φ2dHd-1≥0for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C^\infty (\mathbb {S}^{d-1})$$\end{document}φ∈C∞(Sd-1). Finally, by the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _s(\Sigma _w)$$\end{document}λs(Σw), it is immediate to see that (5.3) is equivalent to the claimed lower bound (5.2). □ □

In light of the stability condition for positive exponents [33, Theorem 4.1], the previous result can be extended to the whole range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (-2,2)$$\end{document}γ∈(-2,2). Naturally, in the case of non-negative exponents, the weighted eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _s(\Sigma _w)$$\end{document}λs(Σw) admits alternative formulations depending on the notion of stability exploited in the proof (see the discussion in Remark 3.8).

For the case of minimal surfaces, the stability criterion for cones can be stated as follows (see [44]). Let C be a minimal cone in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd, smooth outside the origin, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M:= C\cap \mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}M:=C∩Sd-1 is smooth in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1. Consider the eigenvalue problem5.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda (M):= \min _{\begin{array}{c} \phi \in C^\infty (\mathbb {S}^{d-1})\\ \phi \not \equiv 0 \end{array}}\frac{\displaystyle \int _{M} \left( |\nabla _M \phi |^2 -|A_M|^2 \phi ^2\right) \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-2}}{\displaystyle \int _{M} \phi ^2 \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-2}}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Λ(M):=minϕ∈C∞(Sd-1)ϕ≢0∫M|∇Mϕ|2-|AM|2ϕ2dHd-2∫Mϕ2dHd-2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|A_M|^2$$\end{document}|AM|2 is the squared norm of the second fundamental form of M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _M$$\end{document}∇M is the tangential gradient on M. Then, C is stable in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd if and only if5.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda (M)\ge -\left( \frac{d-3}{2}\right) ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Λ(M)≥-d-322.The eigenvalue Λ (M)Λ(M) is the first eigenvalue of Jacobi operator associated to M. Indeed, if we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_M:=\Delta _M + |A_M|^2$$\end{document}LM:=ΔM+|AM|2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _M$$\end{document}ΔM is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on M, then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi :\mathbb {S}^{d-1}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ϕ:Sd-1→R satisfying\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ -L_M \phi = \Lambda (M) \phi \quad \text{ on } M,\qquad \int _M \phi ^2 \,d\mathcal {H}^{d-2}=1. $$\end{document}-LMϕ=Λ(M)ϕonM,∫Mϕ2dHd-2=1.

Exploring the limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow -2$$\end{document}γ→-2

In order to provide some insight into the limit of the stability criterion as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow -2$$\end{document}γ→-2, we begin by sketching the phenomena that arise in this singular regime. The key point in this discussion is the behavior of the weighted measure associated with (5.1) as s → -1s→-1 (see (5.7) below).

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_k \rightarrow -1$$\end{document}sk→-1, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_k$$\end{document}wk be a sequence of 1-homogeneous global stable solutions, regular outside the origin, in the sense of Definition 3.2 and Definition 2.1, such that5.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w_k|_{\mathbb {S}^{d-1}} \in C^{2,\alpha }(\overline{\Sigma }_{w_k}) \text { and } \Sigma _{w_k}:=\Omega _{w_k}\cap \mathbb {S}^{d-1} \text { is } C^{2,\alpha }\text { -regular, uniformly in } k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}wk|Sd-1∈C2,α(Σ¯wk)andΣwk:=Ωwk∩Sd-1isC2,α-regular, uniformly ink.The results in Sect. 2 do not provide estimates that are uniform in k. As far as we know, uniform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,\alpha }$$\end{document}C1,α estimates have been established only in [20], for minimizers of a normalized Alt–Phillips functional. The proof of assumption (5.6) is rather involved and would require a more detailed refinement of our regularity theory, which goes beyond the scope of this paper.

Under such compactness, we proceed by studying the asymptotic behavior of both the Euler–Lagrange equation and the Rayleigh-type quotient corresponding to (5.1), as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}k→+∞. Roughly speaking, the proof consists into showing that5.7where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M:=\lim _{k\rightarrow \infty }\partial \Sigma _{w_k}$$\end{document}M:=limk→∞∂Σwk. The presence of the coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+s_k)$$\end{document}(1+sk) is coherent with the normalization introduced in [19, Theorem 2.4], where they shown that the perimeter functional coincides with Γ Γ-limit of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_s$$\end{document}Es, as s→ -1s→-1. Precisely, in the context of minimizers, they show that in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1$$\end{document}L1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_\infty :=\lim _{k\rightarrow +\infty }w_k$$\end{document}w∞:=limk→+∞wk. Then (5.7) follows by noticing\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |\nabla w_k^{1+s_k}| = (1+s_k)w_k^{s_k} |\nabla w_k|,\qquad \text{ and }\qquad |\nabla w_k|=1\quad \text {on }\partial \Sigma _{w_k}. $$\end{document}|∇wk1+sk|=(1+sk)wkk|∇wk|,and|∇wk|=1on∂Σwk.We also notice that (5.6) allows to deduce that M is minimal, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{M}=0$$\end{document}HM=0. Indeed, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_k$$\end{document}Hk be the mean curvature of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Sigma _{w_k}$$\end{document}∂Σwk oriented towards the complement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{w_k}$$\end{document}Ωwk and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _k$$\end{document}νk be the outer normal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Sigma _{w_k}$$\end{document}∂Σwk. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ w_k(x-t\nu _k(x)) = t - \frac{H_k}{2(1+s_k)}t^2 + O(t^{2+\alpha }),\quad \text{ for } x\in \partial \Sigma _{w_k}. $$\end{document}wk(x-tνk(x))=t-Hk2(1+sk)t2+O(t2+α),forx∈∂Σwk.In light of the higher regularity result of Sect. 2, the computation of the second normal derivative is essentially equivalent to one in [33, Lemma 5.1]. Hence, by substituting such expansion in the free boundary condition (2.1) (alternatively (1.2)), we deduce that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{t \rightarrow 0^+} t^{1+s_k}H_k = 0$$\end{document}limt→0+t1+skHk=0 for every k>0k>0. Therefore, the claim follows by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2,\alpha }$$\end{document}C2,α-compactness.

In the present section we proceed by underlying the effect of such phenomenon by showing the convergence of the stability criterion Proposition 5.1, under the uniform regularity assumptions.

Step 1: the asymptotic of the first eigenfunction. We begin by introducing some notations. We want to conclude by showing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _\infty =\Lambda (M)$$\end{document}λ∞=Λ(M), so that M is a stable minimal cone. A priori, the claim would follow by showing that the Rayleigh-type quotient in (5.1) converges to the one in (5.4) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞. Thus, let us omit the dependence on the index k and consider the same notations introduced above. Remark 5.2

(The quadratic form associated to the stability) In the previous steps, we focused on the asymptotic behavior of homogeneous stable solutions of the Alt–Phillips problem as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow -2$$\end{document}γ→-2, motivated by their relevance in the classification of singular points. However, under the regularity assumption (5.6), this asymptotic analysis can be readily extended to stable solutions regular outside the origin, in the sense of Definition 3.1 and Definition 2.1, by showing that the limit of the associated free boundaries is indeed a stable minimal surface.

Our analysis highlights a deep difference between the asymptotic of stable regular solutions and the one of the quadratic form associated to the stability condition, i.e.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q_s(f):=\int _{ \Omega _w} w^s|\nabla w|^2\left( |\nabla f|^2 - \mathcal {A}^2_w\, f^2\right) \,dx. $$\end{document}Qs(f):=∫Ωwws|∇w|2|∇f|2-Aw2f2dx.An additional term appears in the limit of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_s$$\end{document}Qs as s → -1s→-1, corresponding to the derivative of the test functions in the direction orthogonal to the limiting interface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M:= \lim _k \partial \Omega _{w_k}$$\end{document}M:=limk∂Ωwk. Nevertheless, the stability of this limit interface can be obtained by choosing test functions of type (5.14). This behavior is not unexpected, as it mirrors phenomena observed for the singular limit of stable solutions to the Allen–Cahn equation (see [29, 36]).
